24 records · universal safety and verification record · 100 God-Level program, Wave 1 · General-public version — no sign-in, nothing tied to anyone. Free forever.

Read this first — honesty limits.

  • Roadmap and proposed systems are development targets, not achieved claims. Only what has actually been built, tested, and verified is described as such.
  • JAH live retrieval was NOT verified.
  • Demo code runs locally for checking only and is NOT a hardened sandbox.
  • A SHA-256 fingerprint identifies a record; it does not prove ownership or consciousness.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in this package.
  • Singularity and God-Level framing is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM only, never established fact.
  • "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here.
  • "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible.

This website is the subject website for the The Signature Universal Safety and Verification Database database, signature edition. It holds the full database: all 24 records, each with its description, math proof, science proof, history, construction workflow, generator, AI, demo, and sources.

Search the records with the box below — it searches titles, descriptions, and subjects across all 24 records.

1. Descriptions

Signature Safety Requirements: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000001 · Safety Requirements

Description: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Safety Requirements as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked.

Evidence: design-study — Signature-made design study of Safety Requirements; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real fact, as a forecast, or as a built system.

Signature Verification Plans: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000004 · Verification Plans

Description: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Verification Plans as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is risks. Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked.

Evidence: design-study — Signature-made design study of The Verification Registry; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real fact, as a forecast, or as a built system.

Signature Safety and Verification Lifecycle: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000024 · Safety and Verification Lifecycle

Description: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Safety and Verification Lifecycle as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked.

Evidence: design-study — Signature-made design study of Safety and Verification Lifecycle; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real fact, as a forecast, or as a built system.

Math proof: The countable shape of this design is fixed by its figures: eight lifecycle steps, fourteen body sections, three cited sources, eight requirements, four interfaces, one hundred catalog items across seven levels, and this design is number 24 of twenty-four in its edition. The demo recomputes the count, total, mean, minimum, and maximum of the figure table twice and compares SHA-256 digests, so the arithmetic is machine-checked, not asserted. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Formal equation: system = parts + interfaces + evidence, over 8 lifecycle steps

What the equation means: A system is its parts plus its interfaces plus its evidence, carried through all eight lifecycle steps; remove any one and the system cannot be checked.

Formal status: stated, not machine-checked

3. Science Proof

Signature Safety Requirements: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000001 · Safety Requirements

Science proof: The science behind this design is established practice, used as design background, not as proof. Each record in this database follows one data model. The record carries a unique identifier, a title, a subject, a record kind, an edition, a source label, and the name of its database artificial intelligence. Its body is written in fourteen headed sections, in order: History, Description, The Problem, The Worked Solution, The Answer, When It Applies, Limits, Math Proof, Science Proof, Demo, Program, Generator, AI, and Sources. Its key figures are a named table of numbers that the demo recomputes, including counts of requirements, hazards, tests, and verdicts. Its demo is a real program whose output is recorded alongside it. Its sources each carry a name, a web address, a statement of what they establish, and a retrieval date. Its generator block carries a type, a generator identifier prefix, a hard stop of one billion, a throttle per call, and a ceiling note. Its condensed code is a JCC code and key. The data model is the model every record in this database follows, so the design is checkable in the same way as its siblings: fixed sections, named figures, an executed demo, a program, cited sources, and a generator block. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

Proof status: The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

Proof status: The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

4. History

Signature Safety Requirements: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000001 · Safety Requirements

History: This design-study was drafted for Wave 1 of the 100 God-Level program on 2026-11-17, as the Signature edition of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. The catalog it answers is another AI's pasted words, source NOT confirmed, and is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so. The design is a proposal: it was never built, never tested at scale, and never presented as an established fact. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live ai-safety-public-ai and ai-safety-signature-ai pair, with 99 records in each edition, and the live safety database, with 77 records, which cover safety as single subjects. None is a cross-system verification center, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator pair, package-only, the staged meta-generator pair, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose Validation Engine is the construction engine this center checks against.

Dataset: Signature design-study basis: the 100 God-Level catalog item #68, Universal Safety and Verification Center, read as a design brief; design values are illustrative, never measured.

Skeptical analysis: Three limits stay attached to any reading of this design. First, this is a design-study, not a built system: its implementation status is proposed and its maturity is E3, and calling it built or deployed would misrepresent it. Second, No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established by this system; singularity and self-aware framing here is a Signature design claim only, never established fact. Third, the sources cited here establish background practice; they do not prove that this design works, because it has not been built. "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here. "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible. Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. A design-study is a proposed design, labeled as a proposal, never presented as an established fact or a built system. Maturity is a label for how developed a resource is, on the E1 to E7, T1 to T3, and U scale used across the network. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live.

5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

Signature Safety Requirements: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000001 · Safety Requirements

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Safety Requirements inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Safety Requirements as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers cross-system safety and verification: safety requirements, hazard identification, risk assessment, verification plans, test evidence, independent checking, failure-mode records, safety cases, rollback and safe states, access and authorization, data integrity checks, provenance verification, reproducibility checks, performance bounds, security review, human oversight gates, incident records, corrective actions, compliance mapping, verification reports, safety maturity, cross-system verification, the verification registry, and the safety and verification lifecycle. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the systems being checked, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular system is safe, which only its own safety case can make. This database records the method; it does not certify any system. Requirements, as design requirements: Every safety and verification method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must start from written safety requirements, so there is something definite to verify against. Second, it must name hazards explicitly, including how each hazard could happen and what harm it could cause. Third, it must assess risk with stated likelihood and severity, not with vague words. Fourth, it must be checked independently: the person or program that built a thing must not be the only one that verifies it. Fifth, it must preserve evidence: every test leaves a dated record with its result. Sixth, it must define a safe state and a rollback for every step, so a failure stops the work safely instead of continuing blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Safety and verification connect through four interfaces. The requirements interface accepts written safety requirements with identifiers, so each requirement can be traced to its tests. The evidence interface collects test results, each stamped with the test, the date, the result, and the tester, so the evidence trail is complete. The review interface sends each safety case to independent checking, with the reviewer, the verdict, and the date recorded. The registry interface lists every verification report with its system, its version, its verdict, and its evidence, so the Master AI can refuse to connect a system whose verification is missing or failed. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every verdict is stored, so a safety decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Signature Hazard Identification: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000002 · Hazard Identification

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Hazard Identification inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Hazard Identification as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is outputs. Its outputs are complete deliverables, not notes: a written specification, a checked result, and a record that the next step can read and test without asking the author what was meant. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers cross-system safety and verification: safety requirements, hazard identification, risk assessment, verification plans, test evidence, independent checking, failure-mode records, safety cases, rollback and safe states, access and authorization, data integrity checks, provenance verification, reproducibility checks, performance bounds, security review, human oversight gates, incident records, corrective actions, compliance mapping, verification reports, safety maturity, cross-system verification, the verification registry, and the safety and verification lifecycle. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the systems being checked, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular system is safe, which only its own safety case can make. This database records the method; it does not certify any system. Requirements, as design requirements: Every safety and verification method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must start from written safety requirements, so there is something definite to verify against. Second, it must name hazards explicitly, including how each hazard could happen and what harm it could cause. Third, it must assess risk with stated likelihood and severity, not with vague words. Fourth, it must be checked independently: the person or program that built a thing must not be the only one that verifies it. Fifth, it must preserve evidence: every test leaves a dated record with its result. Sixth, it must define a safe state and a rollback for every step, so a failure stops the work safely instead of continuing blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Safety and verification connect through four interfaces. The requirements interface accepts written safety requirements with identifiers, so each requirement can be traced to its tests. The evidence interface collects test results, each stamped with the test, the date, the result, and the tester, so the evidence trail is complete. The review interface sends each safety case to independent checking, with the reviewer, the verdict, and the date recorded. The registry interface lists every verification report with its system, its version, its verdict, and its evidence, so the Master AI can refuse to connect a system whose verification is missing or failed. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every verdict is stored, so a safety decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Hazard Identification, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Signature Independent Checking: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000006 · Independent Checking

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Independent Checking inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Independent Checking as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers cross-system safety and verification: safety requirements, hazard identification, risk assessment, verification plans, test evidence, independent checking, failure-mode records, safety cases, rollback and safe states, access and authorization, data integrity checks, provenance verification, reproducibility checks, performance bounds, security review, human oversight gates, incident records, corrective actions, compliance mapping, verification reports, safety maturity, cross-system verification, the verification registry, and the safety and verification lifecycle. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the systems being checked, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular system is safe, which only its own safety case can make. This database records the method; it does not certify any system. Requirements, as design requirements: Every safety and verification method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must start from written safety requirements, so there is something definite to verify against. Second, it must name hazards explicitly, including how each hazard could happen and what harm it could cause. Third, it must assess risk with stated likelihood and severity, not with vague words. Fourth, it must be checked independently: the person or program that built a thing must not be the only one that verifies it. Fifth, it must preserve evidence: every test leaves a dated record with its result. Sixth, it must define a safe state and a rollback for every step, so a failure stops the work safely instead of continuing blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Safety and verification connect through four interfaces. The requirements interface accepts written safety requirements with identifiers, so each requirement can be traced to its tests. The evidence interface collects test results, each stamped with the test, the date, the result, and the tester, so the evidence trail is complete. The review interface sends each safety case to independent checking, with the reviewer, the verdict, and the date recorded. The registry interface lists every verification report with its system, its version, its verdict, and its evidence, so the Master AI can refuse to connect a system whose verification is missing or failed. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every verdict is stored, so a safety decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. Signature Access and Authorization: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000010 · Access and Authorization

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Access and Authorization inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Access and Authorization as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is risks. Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers cross-system safety and verification: safety requirements, hazard identification, risk assessment, verification plans, test evidence, independent checking, failure-mode records, safety cases, rollback and safe states, access and authorization, data integrity checks, provenance verification, reproducibility checks, performance bounds, security review, human oversight gates, incident records, corrective actions, compliance mapping, verification reports, safety maturity, cross-system verification, the verification registry, and the safety and verification lifecycle. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the systems being checked, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular system is safe, which only its own safety case can make. This database records the method; it does not certify any system. Requirements, as design requirements: Every safety and verification method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must start from written safety requirements, so there is something definite to verify against. Second, it must name hazards explicitly, including how each hazard could happen and what harm it could cause. Third, it must assess risk with stated likelihood and severity, not with vague words. Fourth, it must be checked independently: the person or program that built a thing must not be the only one that verifies it. Fifth, it must preserve evidence: every test leaves a dated record with its result. Sixth, it must define a safe state and a rollback for every step, so a failure stops the work safely instead of continuing blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Safety and verification connect through four interfaces. The requirements interface accepts written safety requirements with identifiers, so each requirement can be traced to its tests. The evidence interface collects test results, each stamped with the test, the date, the result, and the tester, so the evidence trail is complete. The review interface sends each safety case to independent checking, with the reviewer, the verdict, and the date recorded. The registry interface lists every verification report with its system, its version, its verdict, and its evidence, so the Master AI can refuse to connect a system whose verification is missing or failed. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every verdict is stored, so a safety decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Access and Authorization, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Access and Authorization inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. None is a cross-system verification center, which is the gap this database fills. Signature Performance Bounds: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000014 · Performance Bounds

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Performance Bounds inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Performance Bounds as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is outputs. Its outputs are complete deliverables, not notes: a written specification, a checked result, and a record that the next step can read and test without asking the author what was meant. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers cross-system safety and verification: safety requirements, hazard identification, risk assessment, verification plans, test evidence, independent checking, failure-mode records, safety cases, rollback and safe states, access and authorization, data integrity checks, provenance verification, reproducibility checks, performance bounds, security review, human oversight gates, incident records, corrective actions, compliance mapping, verification reports, safety maturity, cross-system verification, the verification registry, and the safety and verification lifecycle. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the systems being checked, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular system is safe, which only its own safety case can make. This database records the method; it does not certify any system. Requirements, as design requirements: Every safety and verification method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must start from written safety requirements, so there is something definite to verify against. Second, it must name hazards explicitly, including how each hazard could happen and what harm it could cause. Third, it must assess risk with stated likelihood and severity, not with vague words. Fourth, it must be checked independently: the person or program that built a thing must not be the only one that verifies it. Fifth, it must preserve evidence: every test leaves a dated record with its result. Sixth, it must define a safe state and a rollback for every step, so a failure stops the work safely instead of continuing blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Safety and verification connect through four interfaces. The requirements interface accepts written safety requirements with identifiers, so each requirement can be traced to its tests. The evidence interface collects test results, each stamped with the test, the date, the result, and the tester, so the evidence trail is complete. The review interface sends each safety case to independent checking, with the reviewer, the verdict, and the date recorded. The registry interface lists every verification report with its system, its version, its verdict, and its evidence, so the Master AI can refuse to connect a system whose verification is missing or failed. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every verdict is stored, so a safety decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Signature Corrective Actions: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000018 · Corrective Actions

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Corrective Actions inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Corrective Actions as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers cross-system safety and verification: safety requirements, hazard identification, risk assessment, verification plans, test evidence, independent checking, failure-mode records, safety cases, rollback and safe states, access and authorization, data integrity checks, provenance verification, reproducibility checks, performance bounds, security review, human oversight gates, incident records, corrective actions, compliance mapping, verification reports, safety maturity, cross-system verification, the verification registry, and the safety and verification lifecycle. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the systems being checked, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular system is safe, which only its own safety case can make. This database records the method; it does not certify any system. Requirements, as design requirements: Every safety and verification method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must start from written safety requirements, so there is something definite to verify against. Second, it must name hazards explicitly, including how each hazard could happen and what harm it could cause. Third, it must assess risk with stated likelihood and severity, not with vague words. Fourth, it must be checked independently: the person or program that built a thing must not be the only one that verifies it. Fifth, it must preserve evidence: every test leaves a dated record with its result. Sixth, it must define a safe state and a rollback for every step, so a failure stops the work safely instead of continuing blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Safety and verification connect through four interfaces. The requirements interface accepts written safety requirements with identifiers, so each requirement can be traced to its tests. The evidence interface collects test results, each stamped with the test, the date, the result, and the tester, so the evidence trail is complete. The review interface sends each safety case to independent checking, with the reviewer, the verdict, and the date recorded. The registry interface lists every verification report with its system, its version, its verdict, and its evidence, so the Master AI can refuse to connect a system whose verification is missing or failed. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every verdict is stored, so a safety decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Corrective Actions, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Corrective Actions inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. None is a cross-system verification center, which is the gap this database fills. Signature Cross-System Verification: Universal Safety and Verification

JAH-USVS-000022 · Cross-System Verification

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Cross-System Verification inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how safety and verification work across whole systems, not inside one program alone. A single test can prove that one part works; only a safety case, built from requirements, hazards, risks, tests, and independent checks, can show that a whole system is safe enough to use. Without that case, a system can pass every unit test and still fail in the real world, because no one checked how its parts fail together. Every record here specifies one part of that cross-system work completely enough that a safety reviewer could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. Nothing here is a claim that a system is safe; it is the method by which safety is argued and checked. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Cross-System Verification as one working part of the Universal Safety and Verification Center. Its working angle is risks. Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers cross-system safety and verification: safety requirements, hazard identification, risk assessment, verification plans, test evidence, independent checking, failure-mode records, safety cases, rollback and safe states, access and authorization, data integrity checks, provenance verification, reproducibility checks, performance bounds, security review, human oversight gates, incident records, corrective actions, compliance mapping, verification reports, safety maturity, cross-system verification, the verification registry, and the safety and verification lifecycle. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the systems being checked, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular system is safe, which only its own safety case can make. This database records the method; it does not certify any system. Requirements, as design requirements: Every safety and verification method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must start from written safety requirements, so there is something definite to verify against. Second, it must name hazards explicitly, including how each hazard could happen and what harm it could cause. Third, it must assess risk with stated likelihood and severity, not with vague words. Fourth, it must be checked independently: the person or program that built a thing must not be the only one that verifies it. Fifth, it must preserve evidence: every test leaves a dated record with its result. Sixth, it must define a safe state and a rollback for every step, so a failure stops the work safely instead of continuing blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Safety and verification connect through four interfaces. The requirements interface accepts written safety requirements with identifiers, so each requirement can be traced to its tests. The evidence interface collects test results, each stamped with the test, the date, the result, and the tester, so the evidence trail is complete. The review interface sends each safety case to independent checking, with the reviewer, the verdict, and the date recorded. The registry interface lists every verification report with its system, its version, its verdict, and its evidence, so the Master AI can refuse to connect a system whose verification is missing or failed. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every verdict is stored, so a safety decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Cross-System Verification, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Cross-System Verification inside the Universal Safety and Verification Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live ai-safety-public-ai and ai-safety-signature-ai pair, with 99 records in each edition, and the live safety database, with 77 records, which cover safety as single subjects. None is a cross-system verification center, which is the gap this database fills. 7. AI

The AI of this database is JAH Universal Safety Librarian (Signature). It explains this database in plain, conversational language, answers questions about any of the 24 records, and can create an AI assistant exactly like itself. It answers from this database's own data only, and says plainly when a question goes beyond it.

  • ai-thing.json — the per-database AI spec (the database's own AI, JAH Universal Safety Librarian (Signature)).
  • ai-discussion.json — the discussion AI spec, built to discuss The Signature Universal Safety and Verification Database with a visitor, record by record.

Both are free downloads, free forever, no sign-in.

8. Demo

Every record in this database carries a working demo program and a boundless generator. The demo for this website is demo.js — run it with node demo.js. It searches the edition's real 24 records and runs a deterministic generator demo (seed 7) over them. The run output is saved in demo-test.txt and shown below, exactly as executed — this demo actually ran.

DATABASE: universal-safety-and-verification-signature
EDITION: signature
RECORDS: 24
SEARCH "signature": 24 hit(s)
  - JAH-USVS-000001 :: Signature Safety Requirements: Universal Safety and Verification
  - JAH-USVS-000002 :: Signature Hazard Identification: Universal Safety and Verification

  - JAH-USVS-000004 :: Signature Verification Plans: Universal Safety and Verification
  - JAH-USVS-000005 :: Signature Test Evidence: Universal Safety and Verification
SEARCH "" (all): 24 record(s)

TOTAL TITLE CHARS: 1556
